And during that same stretch, I’ve also had the voices and times where it’s harder to keep my thoughts straight, like I get tangled up trying to explain myself.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say roughly eleven months.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having voices, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yes… by voices, I don’t mean like a whole conversation going on all day or anything like that. It’s more… it happens sometimes when the house is very quiet, especially if I’m alone and there isn’t any television or music on.\n\nI’ll hear my name, clear enough that I look up or even answer, like, “What?” And then I remember nobody is there. Sometimes it’s just a few words, not a long sentence. The strange part is it can sound like my friend’s voice, like it’s coming from the kitchen or maybe the porch. It’s not muffled the way you’d think if I was just imagining it — it feels clear in that moment, like someone really called from the other room.\n\nThen I’ll go still and listen, and of course there’s no one there. I feel embarrassed even saying it, because I know how it sounds, but it’s happened enough times that I can’t just brush it off as a one-time mistake. It startles me more than anything. Sometimes I answer before I even have time to think, and then I feel silly standing there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or influence your actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of giving me instructions, no. It’s not like a voice says, “Go do this,” or “Don’t do that.” It’s more that I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it catches me off guard.\n\nUsually it’s when the house is very quiet, and I’ll think I hear my friend’s voice from the kitchen or the porch, clear enough that I might answer before I remember nobody is there. Like I’ll say, “What?” and then I’m standing there listening, and there’s nothing. That’s the part that bothers me, how real it seems for a second.\n\nI suppose it influences me only in that I stop what I’m doing and check, or I get a little unsettled afterward. But it doesn’t tell me to do things. It’s more startling than commanding.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of being watched and the messages you think you're receiving seem to be part of a larger story or idea that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of what scares me, because it does start to feel like it all fits together, even when I tell myself maybe I’m misunderstanding.\n\nIt’s mostly connected to people at church and in my walking club. On better days, I can still go and act normal enough, but I can’t shake this feeling that certain people are watching me more closely than they should be. Like they notice where I sit, who I talk to, whether I missed a Sunday, little things like that.\n\nAnd then I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a line that seems too close to something about me, and I think, “Why would they put that in there unless it was meant for me?” Or someone in the walking club will say something kind of casual, but the way they say it feels pointed, like there’s a hidden message underneath it. Not always direct, but enough that I feel like they’re trying to tell me they know things about me or they’re discussing me without saying it outright.\n\nI know if I say it out loud it sounds like maybe I’m reading too much into things. I do know that. But in the moment, it feels connected, like the bulletin and the comments and the watching are all part of the same thing. Even when I’m having a better day, I can’t completely talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you don't feel like you're being watched or that messages are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can distract myself for a little while, yes. If I’m busy with something very ordinary, like washing dishes or talking to someone about nothing important, I may not be thinking about it every minute.\n\nBut honestly, even on my better days, it’s still there in the background. That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t seem to fully shake this feeling that some people at church or in the walking club are watching me, sort of keeping track of me. And then little things feel like messages — something in the bulletin, or a comment someone makes while we’re walking — and I start thinking it was put there because of me.\n\nI know there are moments when it’s quieter in my mind, but I don’t really have days where I feel completely free of it. It’s more like I can push it aside for a bit, and then something small happens and it all comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to express your thoughts in a way that feels clear and understandable to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I don’t always notice it right away while I’m talking, but then I see the person’s face, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t.\n\nI’ll start off trying to explain one simple thing, like something about the mail or something that happened at church, and in my mind it all feels connected, so I keep going. But then somehow I’m talking about a quilt pattern I saw, or the birds outside my window, or how something felt like a warning, and I realize I’ve gone far away from where I started.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. I can feel the point, if that makes sense. But getting it out in a straight line is hard. I’ll be saying, “Well, the bulletin said this,” and then I remember the envelope in the mail, and then the colors on the quilt, and then the birds were all gathered in a way that seemed important, and by then the person is just looking at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at.\n\nSo yes, I think people understand pieces of what I say, but not always the whole thing. And then I get embarrassed and try to explain more, which sometimes makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think all these things - the watching, the messages, the voices - are connected in some way that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… the watching and the messages, those feel connected to me. It’s more little things that add up. Someone notices whether I’m there or not, or looks at me a little too long, or says something during the walk that seems casual but feels like it has another meaning. And then the church bulletin will have a phrase in it that feels too close to something about me, and I start thinking, “That was put there for me to see.”\n\nI know part of me can say maybe I’m making connections that aren’t really there. I do understand that’s possible. But it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening. It feels like the watching and the little messages are part of the same thing, like people are keeping track of me and letting me know they know. That’s the part I can’t seem to talk myself out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I think that happens to me quite a bit, though I don’t always realize it until I’m already halfway into it.\n\nI might start out trying to tell someone something simple, like about a piece of mail I got, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it clearly. But then one thought sort of hooks onto another one. The mail makes me think of the bulletin, and then the bulletin makes me think of a quilt pattern I was working on, because of the colors or the shapes, and then I remember the birds outside and how they were gathered in a strange way, and before I know it I’m talking about that instead.\n\nIn my head, it feels connected while I’m saying it. It feels like I’m still on the same subject, in a way. But then I look at the other person and they have that blank look, or they ask me, “Wait, what do you mean?” And I realize they don’t know how I got from the first thing to the last thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I don’t mean to wander off like that. I really am trying to make a point, but sometimes I end up going through all these side paths before I can get there, and then I’m not even sure I’ve gotten there at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to keep it in order, I really do, but it’s like the order makes sense inside my head and then it doesn’t come out that way.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, like maybe I’m explaining a letter that came in the mail, or something from church, and I think I’m being clear. But then there’s some detail that reminds me of something else, like the colors in a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were sitting outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning. And then I follow that thought because it feels important, and before I know it I’m not talking about the mail anymore.\n\nThen I look at the person’s face and I can tell they’ve lost me. Or I’ve lost them, I guess. They’re waiting for me to get back to the point, but by then I’m not always sure how to get back neatly. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something, or is this something that has developed over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can put it neatly on a date like that. I don’t remember always being this way, not in the same way, but I also don’t want to say for sure exactly when it changed.\n\nWhat I notice now is that when I try to explain something, I’ll start with one clear thing, like the mail or something that happened at church, and then I get pulled into all these other pieces. I’ll mention a quilt pattern because it seems connected somehow, or the birds outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning, and while I’m talking it feels like I’m following the thread.\n\nBut then I can see the other person’s face, and they don’t know what I’m getting at. They’ll look confused, or they’ll ask me to go back, and then I realize I’ve gone somewhere else entirely. So whether it started exactly eleven months ago, I’m not sure, but it’s been much more of a problem lately, or at least I’m noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something to someone?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… at least I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a little more than some people, and I can get caught up in details, but this thing where I’m trying to explain one simple thing and it sort of wanders all over, that feels more noticeable now.\n\nLike I’ll start out telling someone about the mail I got, or something from church, and I really do think I’m staying with the point. But then something in it reminds me of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were outside, or some feeling like there was a warning in it, and I follow that because it seems connected at the time. Then after a while I can see on the person’s face that they don’t know what I’m getting at anymore.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve always had that trouble in the same way. It’s just… when it happens now, it gets away from me before I notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Then after a while I can see on the person’s face that they don’t know what I’m getting at anymore.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve always had that trouble in the same way. It’s just… when it happens now, it gets away from me before I notice.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you used to be able to explain things clearly to others without getting sidetracked before these feelings of being watched started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I did better before. I mean, I was never a perfect storyteller or anything, I could always take a long way around a point, but I used to be able to explain normal things and have people follow me.

Now it’s different. I’ll start out trying to say something simple, like about a letter that came in the mail, or something that happened at church, and then my mind catches on another piece of it. I’ll think of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were lined up outside, or something that felt like a warning, and I’ll start talking about that because in the moment it feels connected.

But then I’ll see the other person’s face, and they’re just sort of waiting, like they don’t know where I’m going with it. And then I realize I’ve gotten sidetracked and I don’t know how to get back to the beginning without sounding foolish. So yes, I used to be clearer than I am now.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F28\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I’ve been having this feeling that I just can’t seem to shake, even when I’m having a pretty good day otherwise. It’s mostly around people from church, and also the walking club I go to. I keep feeling like certain people are watching me, not in an obvious way exactly, but like they’re keeping track of me.\n\nAnd then little things start to seem like they’re about me. Like something written in the church bulletin, or a comment someone makes in passing during the walk, and I’ll think, “That was meant for me,” or “They’re trying to send a message about me without saying it directly.” I know it probably sounds strange, and I’ve tried to tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into things, but it keeps happening, and it’s been upsetting enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who you feel are watching you are working together or is it more individual?\n\nPatient: It feels more like… connected, I guess. I don’t know that I can prove they’re all sitting down together and planning it, but it doesn’t feel completely random or just one person, either. It’s like certain people at church and then certain people from the walking club seem to pick up on the same thing.\n\nLike I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a little note, and it feels like it’s meant for me, like they put it there so I’d know they were aware of me. And then later, during the walking group, someone will say something in passing that seems to line up with it, not direct enough that I could point to it and say, “There, that’s it,” but enough that I get that feeling again, like they’re watching me or keeping track.\n\nI know that sounds… I know how it sounds. I try to tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into it. But when it happens in both places, church and the walking club, it’s hard not to feel like there’s some kind of connection between them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you think are being sent to you are in any way unusual or impossible for others to understand?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes and no. I mean, I know other people might not understand why a church bulletin would feel like it’s talking about me, or why some offhand comment in the walking club would seem connected. If I tried to explain it to someone, they might say, “Well, that’s just a normal announcement,” or “They were just making conversation.”\n\nBut to me, in the moment, it feels pointed. Like even on days when I’m doing all right otherwise, I can’t quite shake this feeling that certain people are watching me, sort of keeping tabs, and then those little things — a phrase in the bulletin, something someone says during the walk — feel like messages about me. Not loud or obvious, but like they’re meant for me to notice.\n\nI do realize it probably sounds unusual to someone else. That’s part of why I’m here, because I can hear how it might sound, but it still keeps feeling real to me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these messages you think are being sent to you are threatening in any way?\n\nPatient: Not threatening like they’re going to hurt me, exactly… I don’t usually think that. It’s more that it feels uncomfortable and pointed, like they’re watching me and trying to let me know they know things about me, or they’re talking about me without saying my name.\n\nLike with the church bulletin, it might just be a phrase or a little note, but I’ll read it and think, “That’s about me.” And then later someone in the walking club says something that seems to match up with it, and I get this sinking feeling, like it isn’t just coincidence. It doesn’t feel openly dangerous, but it does feel unsettling, like I’m being monitored or singled out.\n\nEven on days when I’m otherwise doing okay, I can’t shake it. So I wouldn’t say threatening in a direct way, but it does make me feel uneasy and guarded.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of being watched and receiving hidden messages?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eleven months now, I think. Not quite a full year, but close enough that it feels like it’s been going on for a long time.\n\nAt first I wasn’t sure what to make of it, and I kept thinking maybe it was just me being too sensitive or reading things wrong. But the feeling of being watched, and then those messages or little signs that seem like they’re meant for me, that’s been happening for around eleven months. And during that same stretch, I’ve also had the voices and times where it’s harder to keep my thoughts straight, like I get tangled up trying to explain myself.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say roughly eleven months.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having voices, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yes… by voices, I don’t mean like a whole conversation going on all day or anything like that. It’s more… it happens sometimes when the house is very quiet, especially if I’m alone and there isn’t any television or music on.\n\nI’ll hear my name, clear enough that I look up or even answer, like, “What?” And then I remember nobody is there. Sometimes it’s just a few words, not a long sentence. The strange part is it can sound like my friend’s voice, like it’s coming from the kitchen or maybe the porch. It’s not muffled the way you’d think if I was just imagining it — it feels clear in that moment, like someone really called from the other room.\n\nThen I’ll go still and listen, and of course there’s no one there. I feel embarrassed even saying it, because I know how it sounds, but it’s happened enough times that I can’t just brush it off as a one-time mistake. It startles me more than anything. Sometimes I answer before I even have time to think, and then I feel silly standing there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or influence your actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of giving me instructions, no. It’s not like a voice says, “Go do this,” or “Don’t do that.” It’s more that I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it catches me off guard.\n\nUsually it’s when the house is very quiet, and I’ll think I hear my friend’s voice from the kitchen or the porch, clear enough that I might answer before I remember nobody is there. Like I’ll say, “What?” and then I’m standing there listening, and there’s nothing. That’s the part that bothers me, how real it seems for a second.\n\nI suppose it influences me only in that I stop what I’m doing and check, or I get a little unsettled afterward. But it doesn’t tell me to do things. It’s more startling than commanding.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of being watched and the messages you think you're receiving seem to be part of a larger story or idea that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of what scares me, because it does start to feel like it all fits together, even when I tell myself maybe I’m misunderstanding.\n\nIt’s mostly connected to people at church and in my walking club. On better days, I can still go and act normal enough, but I can’t shake this feeling that certain people are watching me more closely than they should be. Like they notice where I sit, who I talk to, whether I missed a Sunday, little things like that.\n\nAnd then I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a line that seems too close to something about me, and I think, “Why would they put that in there unless it was meant for me?” Or someone in the walking club will say something kind of casual, but the way they say it feels pointed, like there’s a hidden message underneath it. Not always direct, but enough that I feel like they’re trying to tell me they know things about me or they’re discussing me without saying it outright.\n\nI know if I say it out loud it sounds like maybe I’m reading too much into things. I do know that. But in the moment, it feels connected, like the bulletin and the comments and the watching are all part of the same thing. Even when I’m having a better day, I can’t completely talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you don't feel like you're being watched or that messages are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can distract myself for a little while, yes. If I’m busy with something very ordinary, like washing dishes or talking to someone about nothing important, I may not be thinking about it every minute.\n\nBut honestly, even on my better days, it’s still there in the background. That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t seem to fully shake this feeling that some people at church or in the walking club are watching me, sort of keeping track of me. And then little things feel like messages — something in the bulletin, or a comment someone makes while we’re walking — and I start thinking it was put there because of me.\n\nI know there are moments when it’s quieter in my mind, but I don’t really have days where I feel completely free of it. It’s more like I can push it aside for a bit, and then something small happens and it all comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to express your thoughts in a way that feels clear and understandable to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I don’t always notice it right away while I’m talking, but then I see the person’s face, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t.\n\nI’ll start off trying to explain one simple thing, like something about the mail or something that happened at church, and in my mind it all feels connected, so I keep going. But then somehow I’m talking about a quilt pattern I saw, or the birds outside my window, or how something felt like a warning, and I realize I’ve gone far away from where I started.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. I can feel the point, if that makes sense. But getting it out in a straight line is hard. I’ll be saying, “Well, the bulletin said this,” and then I remember the envelope in the mail, and then the colors on the quilt, and then the birds were all gathered in a way that seemed important, and by then the person is just looking at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at.\n\nSo yes, I think people understand pieces of what I say, but not always the whole thing. And then I get embarrassed and try to explain more, which sometimes makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think all these things - the watching, the messages, the voices - are connected in some way that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… the watching and the messages, those feel connected to me. That’s really why I came in, because even when I’m having what I’d call a better day, when I can still go out and be polite and do the normal things, I can’t shake the feeling that certain people at church and in the walking club are watching me.\n\nIt’s not like one big thing happened all at once. It’s more little things that add up. Someone notices whether I’m there or not, or looks at me a little too long, or says something during the walk that seems casual but feels like it has another meaning. And then the church bulletin will have a phrase in it that feels too close to something about me, and I start thinking, “That was put there for me to see.”\n\nI know part of me can say maybe I’m making connections that aren’t really there. I do understand that’s possible. But it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening. It feels like the watching and the little messages are part of the same thing, like people are keeping track of me and letting me know they know. That’s the part I can’t seem to talk myself out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I think that happens to me quite a bit, though I don’t always realize it until I’m already halfway into it.\n\nI might start out trying to tell someone something simple, like about a piece of mail I got, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it clearly. But then one thought sort of hooks onto another one. The mail makes me think of the bulletin, and then the bulletin makes me think of a quilt pattern I was working on, because of the colors or the shapes, and then I remember the birds outside and how they were gathered in a strange way, and before I know it I’m talking about that instead.\n\nIn my head, it feels connected while I’m saying it. It feels like I’m still on the same subject, in a way. But then I look at the other person and they have that blank look, or they ask me, “Wait, what do you mean?” And I realize they don’t know how I got from the first thing to the last thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I don’t mean to wander off like that. I really am trying to make a point, but sometimes I end up going through all these side paths before I can get there, and then I’m not even sure I’ve gotten there at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to keep it in order, I really do, but it’s like the order makes sense inside my head and then it doesn’t come out that way.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, like maybe I’m explaining a letter that came in the mail, or something from church, and I think I’m being clear. But then there’s some detail that reminds me of something else, like the colors in a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were sitting outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning. And then I follow that thought because it feels important, and before I know it I’m not talking about the mail anymore.\n\nThen I look at the person’s face and I can tell they’ve lost me. I try to tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into it. I mean, I know other people might not understand why a church bulletin would feel like it’s talking about me, or why some offhand comment in the walking club would seem connected. If I tried to explain it to someone, they might say, “Well, that’s just a normal announcement,” or “They were just making conversation.”\n\nBut to me, in the moment, it feels pointed. Like even on days when I’m doing all right otherwise, I can’t quite shake this feeling that certain people are watching me, sort of keeping tabs, and then those little things — a phrase in the bulletin, something someone says during the walk — feel like messages about me. Not loud or obvious, but like they’re meant for me to notice.\n\nI do realize it probably sounds unusual to someone else. That’s part of why I’m here, because I can hear how it might sound, but it still keeps feeling real to me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these messages you think are being sent to you are threatening in any way?\n\nPatient: Not threatening like they’re going to hurt me, exactly… I don’t usually think that. It’s more that it feels uncomfortable and pointed, like they’re watching me and trying to let me know they know things about me, or they’re talking about me without saying my name.\n\nLike with the church bulletin, it might just be a phrase or a little note, but I’ll read it and think, “That’s about me.” And then later someone in the walking club says something that seems to match up with it, and I get this sinking feeling, like it isn’t just coincidence. It doesn’t feel openly dangerous, but it does feel unsettling, like I’m being monitored or singled out.\n\nEven on days when I’m otherwise doing okay, I can’t shake it. So I wouldn’t say threatening in a direct way, but it does make me feel uneasy and guarded.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of being watched and receiving hidden messages?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eleven months now, I think. Not quite a full year, but close enough that it feels like it’s been going on for a long time.\n\nAt first I wasn’t sure what to make of it, and I kept thinking maybe it was just me being too sensitive or reading things wrong. But the feeling of being watched, and then those messages or little signs that seem like they’re meant for me, that’s been happening for around eleven months. And during that same stretch, I’ve also had the voices and times where it’s harder to keep my thoughts straight, like I get tangled up trying to explain myself.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say roughly eleven months.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having voices, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yes… by voices, I don’t mean like a whole conversation going on all day or anything like that. It’s more… it happens sometimes when the house is very quiet, especially if I’m alone and there isn’t any television or music on.\n\nI’ll hear my name, clear enough that I look up or even answer, like, “What?” And then I remember nobody is there. Sometimes it’s just a few words, not a long sentence. The strange part is it can sound like my friend’s voice, like it’s coming from the kitchen or maybe the porch. It’s not muffled the way you’d think if I was just imagining it — it feels clear in that moment, like someone really called from the other room.\n\nThen I’ll go still and listen, and of course there’s no one there. I feel embarrassed even saying it, because I know how it sounds, but it’s happened enough times that I can’t just brush it off as a one-time mistake. It startles me more than anything. Sometimes I answer before I even have time to think, and then I feel silly standing there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or influence your actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of giving me instructions, no. It’s not like a voice says, “Go do this,” or “Don’t do that.” It’s more that I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it catches me off guard.\n\nUsually it’s when the house is very quiet, and I’ll think I hear my friend’s voice from the kitchen or the porch, clear enough that I might answer before I remember nobody is there. Like I’ll say, “What?” and then I’m standing there listening, and there’s nothing. That’s the part that bothers me, how real it seems for a second.\n\nI suppose it influences me only in that I stop what I’m doing and check, or I get a little unsettled afterward. But it doesn’t tell me to do things. It’s more startling than commanding.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of being watched and the messages you think you're receiving seem to be part of a larger story or idea that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of what scares me, because it does start to feel like it all fits together, even when I tell myself maybe I’m misunderstanding.\n\nIt’s mostly connected to people at church and in my walking club. On better days, I can still go and act normal enough, but I can’t shake this feeling that certain people are watching me more closely than they should be. Like they notice where I sit, who I talk to, whether I missed a Sunday, little things like that.\n\nAnd then I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a line that seems too close to something about me, and I think, “Why would they put that in there unless it was meant for me?” Or someone in the walking club will say something kind of casual, but the way they say it feels pointed, like there’s a hidden message underneath it. Not always direct, but enough that I feel like they’re trying to tell me they know things about me or they’re discussing me without saying it outright.\n\nI know if I say it out loud it sounds like maybe I’m reading too much into things. I do know that. But in the moment, it feels connected, like the bulletin and the comments and the watching are all part of the same thing. Even when I’m having a better day, I can’t completely talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you don't feel like you're being watched or that messages are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can distract myself for a little while, yes. If I’m busy with something very ordinary, like washing dishes or talking to someone about nothing important, I may not be thinking about it every minute.\n\nBut honestly, even on my better days, it’s still there in the background. That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t seem to fully shake this feeling that some people at church or in the walking club are watching me, sort of keeping track of me. And then little things feel like messages — something in the bulletin, or a comment someone makes while we’re walking — and I start thinking it was put there because of me.\n\nI know there are moments when it’s quieter in my mind, but I don’t really have days where I feel completely free of it. It’s more like I can push it aside for a bit, and then something small happens and it all comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to express your thoughts in a way that feels clear and understandable to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I don’t always notice it right away while I’m talking, but then I see the person’s face, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t.\n\nI’ll start off trying to explain one simple thing, like something about the mail or something that happened at church, and in my mind it all feels connected, so I keep going. But then somehow I’m talking about a quilt pattern I saw, or the birds outside my window, or how something felt like a warning, and I realize I’ve gone far away from where I started.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. I can feel the point, if that makes sense. But getting it out in a straight line is hard. I’ll be saying, “Well, the bulletin said this,” and then I remember the envelope in the mail, and then the colors on the quilt, and then the birds were all gathered in a way that seemed important, and by then the person is just looking at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at.\n\nSo yes, I think people understand pieces of what I say, but not always the whole thing. And then I get embarrassed and try to explain more, which sometimes makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think all these things - the watching, the messages, the voices - are connected in some way that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… the watching and the messages, those feel connected to me. That’s really why I came in, because even when I’m having what I’d call a better day, when I can still go out and be polite and do the normal things, I can’t shake the feeling that certain people at church and in the walking club are watching me.\n\nIt’s not like one big thing happened all at once. It’s more little things that add up. Someone notices whether I’m there or not, or looks at me a little too long, or says something during the walk that seems casual but feels like it has another meaning. And then the church bulletin will have a phrase in it that feels too close to something about me, and I start thinking, “That was put there for me to see.”\n\nI know part of me can say maybe I’m making connections that aren’t really there. I do understand that’s possible. But it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening. It feels like the watching and the little messages are part of the same thing, like people are keeping track of me and letting me know they know. That’s the part I can’t seem to talk myself out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I think that happens to me quite a bit, though I don’t always realize it until I’m already halfway into it.\n\nI might start out trying to tell someone something simple, like about a piece of mail I got, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it clearly. But then one thought sort of hooks onto another one. The mail makes me think of the bulletin, and then the bulletin makes me think of a quilt pattern I was working on, because of the colors or the shapes, and then I remember the birds outside and how they were gathered in a strange way, and before I know it I’m talking about that instead.\n\nIn my head, it feels connected while I’m saying it. It feels like I’m still on the same subject, in a way. But then I look at the other person and they have that blank look, or they ask me, “Wait, what do you mean?” And I realize they don’t know how I got from the first thing to the last thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I don’t mean to wander off like that. I really am trying to make a point, but sometimes I end up going through all these side paths before I can get there, and then I’m not even sure I’ve gotten there at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to keep it in order, I really do, but it’s like the order makes sense inside my head and then it doesn’t come out that way.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, like maybe I’m explaining a letter that came in the mail, or something from church, and I think I’m being clear. But then there’s some detail that reminds me of something else, like the colors in a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were sitting outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning. And then I follow that thought because it feels important, and before I know it I’m not talking about the mail anymore.\n\nThen I look at the person’s face and I can tell they’ve lost me. Or I’ve lost them, I guess. They’re waiting for me to get back to the point, but by then I’m not always sure how to get back neatly. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something, or is this something that has developed over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can put it neatly on a date like that. I don’t remember always being this way, not in the same way, but I also don’t want to say for sure exactly when it changed.\n\nWhat I notice now is that when I try to explain something, I’ll start with one clear thing, like the mail or something that happened at church, and then I get pulled into all these other pieces. I’ll mention a quilt pattern because it seems connected somehow, or the birds outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning, and while I’m talking it feels like I’m following the thread.\n\nBut then I can see the other person’s face, and they don’t know what I’m getting at. They’ll look confused, or they’ll ask me to go back, and then I realize I’ve gone somewhere else entirely. So whether it started exactly eleven months ago, I’m not sure, but it’s been much more of a problem lately, or at least I’m noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something to someone?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… at least I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a little more than some people, and I can get caught up in details, but this thing where I’m trying to explain one simple thing and it sort of wanders all over, that feels more noticeable now.\n\nLike I’ll start out telling someone about the mail I got, or something from church, and I really do think I’m staying with the point. But then something in it reminds me of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were outside, or some feeling like there was a warning in it, and I follow that because it seems connected at the time. Then after a while I can see on the person’s face that they don’t know what I’m getting at anymore.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve always had that trouble in the same way. It’s just… when it happens now, it gets away from me before I notice.\n\nDoctor: Did you used to be able to explain things clearly to others without getting sidetracked before these feelings of being watched started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I did better before. I mean, I was never a perfect storyteller or anything, I could always take a long way around a point, but I used to be able to explain normal things and have people follow me.\n\nNow it’s different. I’ll start out trying to say something simple, like about a letter that came in the mail, or something that happened at church, and then my mind catches on another piece of it. I’ll think of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were lined up outside, or something that felt like a warning, and I’ll start talking about that because in the moment it feels connected.\n\nBut then I’ll see the other person’s face, and they’re just sort of waiting, like they don’t know where I’m going with it. And then I realize I’ve gotten sidetracked and I don’t know how to get back to the beginning without sounding foolish. So yes, I used to be clearer than I am now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It does seem like it was around the same general time, yes… though I can’t say it was the exact same day or anything like that. I’m not good at lining it up cleanly.

What I noticed is, once I started feeling watched, or maybe paying attention to things in that way, my talking got harder to keep straight. I’ll begin with something ordinary, like trying to tell someone about the mail, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it plainly. But then one part of it will make me think of a quilt pattern, or the birds outside, or some warning I felt was in there, and I follow that because in my mind it belongs with the first thing.

Then after a while I see the person looking at me like they’ve lost the point, and I realize I’ve gone too far off from where I started. So yes, I’d say it started around that same period, or at least it got much more noticeable then.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I’ve been having this feeling that I just can’t seem to shake, even when I’m having a pretty good day otherwise. It’s mostly around people from church, and also the walking club I go to. I keep feeling like certain people are watching me, not in an obvious way exactly, but like they’re keeping track of me.\n\nAnd then little things start to seem like they’re about me. Like something written in the church bulletin, or a comment someone makes in passing during the walk, and I’ll think, “That was meant for me,” or “They’re trying to send a message about me without saying it directly.” I know it probably sounds strange, and I’ve tried to tell myself maybe I’m reading too much into things, but it keeps happening, and it’s been upsetting enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people who you feel are watching you are working together or is it more individual?\n\nPatient: It feels more like… connected, I guess. I don’t know that I can prove they’re all sitting down together and planning it, but it doesn’t feel completely random or just one person, either. It’s like certain people at church and then certain people from the walking club seem to pick up on the same thing.\n\nLike I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a little note, and it feels like it’s meant for me, like they put it there so I’d know they were aware of me. That’s part of why I’m here, because I can hear how it might sound, but it still keeps feeling real to me when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these messages you think are being sent to you are threatening in any way?\n\nPatient: Not threatening like they’re going to hurt me, exactly… I don’t usually think that. It’s more… it happens sometimes when the house is very quiet, especially if I’m alone and there isn’t any television or music on.\n\nI’ll hear my name, clear enough that I look up or even answer, like, “What?” And then I remember nobody is there. Sometimes it’s just a few words, not a long sentence. The strange part is it can sound like my friend’s voice, like it’s coming from the kitchen or maybe the porch. It’s not muffled the way you’d think if I was just imagining it — it feels clear in that moment, like someone really called from the other room.\n\nThen I’ll go still and listen, and of course there’s no one there. I feel embarrassed even saying it, because I know how it sounds, but it’s happened enough times that I can’t just brush it off as a one-time mistake. It startles me more than anything. Sometimes I answer before I even have time to think, and then I feel silly standing there in the quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or influence your actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of giving me instructions, no. It’s not like a voice says, “Go do this,” or “Don’t do that.” It’s more that I’ll hear my name, or just a few words, and it catches me off guard.\n\nUsually it’s when the house is very quiet, and I’ll think I hear my friend’s voice from the kitchen or the porch, clear enough that I might answer before I remember nobody is there. Like I’ll say, “What?” and then I’m standing there listening, and there’s nothing. That’s the part that bothers me, how real it seems for a second.\n\nI suppose it influences me only in that I stop what I’m doing and check, or I get a little unsettled afterward. But it doesn’t tell me to do things. It’s more startling than commanding.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of being watched and the messages you think you're receiving seem to be part of a larger story or idea that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of what scares me, because it does start to feel like it all fits together, even when I tell myself maybe I’m misunderstanding.\n\nIt’s mostly connected to people at church and in my walking club. On better days, I can still go and act normal enough, but I can’t shake this feeling that certain people are watching me more closely than they should be. Like they notice where I sit, who I talk to, whether I missed a Sunday, little things like that.\n\nAnd then I’ll see something in the church bulletin, just a phrase or a line that seems too close to something about me, and I think, “Why would they put that in there unless it was meant for me?” Or someone in the walking club will say something kind of casual, but the way they say it feels pointed, like there’s a hidden message underneath it. Not always direct, but enough that I feel like they’re trying to tell me they know things about me or they’re discussing me without saying it outright.\n\nI know if I say it out loud it sounds like maybe I’m reading too much into things. I do know that. But in the moment, it feels connected, like the bulletin and the comments and the watching are all part of the same thing. Even when I’m having a better day, I can’t completely talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you don't feel like you're being watched or that messages are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can distract myself for a little while, yes. If I’m busy with something very ordinary, like washing dishes or talking to someone about nothing important, I may not be thinking about it every minute.\n\nBut honestly, even on my better days, it’s still there in the background. That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t seem to fully shake this feeling that some people at church or in the walking club are watching me, sort of keeping track of me. And then little things feel like messages — something in the bulletin, or a comment someone makes while we’re walking — and I start thinking it was put there because of me.\n\nI know there are moments when it’s quieter in my mind, but I don’t really have days where I feel completely free of it. It’s more like I can push it aside for a bit, and then something small happens and it all comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to express your thoughts in a way that feels clear and understandable to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I don’t always notice it right away while I’m talking, but then I see the person’s face, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t.\n\nI’ll start off trying to explain one simple thing, like something about the mail or something that happened at church, and in my mind it all feels connected, so I keep going. But then somehow I’m talking about a quilt pattern I saw, or the birds outside my window, or how something felt like a warning, and I realize I’ve gone far away from where I started.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. I can feel the point, if that makes sense. But getting it out in a straight line is hard. I’ll be saying, “Well, the bulletin said this,” and then I remember the envelope in the mail, and then the colors on the quilt, and then the birds were all gathered in a way that seemed important, and by then the person is just looking at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at.\n\nSo yes, I think people understand pieces of what I say, but not always the whole thing. And then I get embarrassed and try to explain more, which sometimes makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think all these things - the watching, the messages, the voices - are connected in some way that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… the watching and the messages, those feel connected to me. That’s really why I came in, because even when I’m having what I’d call a better day, when I can still go out and be polite and do the normal things, I can’t shake the feeling that certain people at church and in the walking club are watching me.\n\nIt’s not like one big thing happened all at once. It’s more little things that add up. Someone notices whether I’m there or not, or looks at me a little too long, or says something during the walk that seems casual but feels like it has another meaning. It feels like the watching and the little messages are part of the same thing, like people are keeping track of me and letting me know they know. That’s the part I can’t seem to talk myself out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I think that happens to me quite a bit, though I don’t always realize it until I’m already halfway into it.\n\nI might start out trying to tell someone something simple, like about a piece of mail I got, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it clearly. But then one thought sort of hooks onto another one. The mail makes me think of the bulletin, and then the bulletin makes me think of a quilt pattern I was working on, because of the colors or the shapes, and then I remember the birds outside and how they were gathered in a strange way, and before I know it I’m talking about that instead.\n\nIn my head, it feels connected while I’m saying it. It feels like I’m still on the same subject, in a way. But then I look at the other person and they have that blank look, or they ask me, “Wait, what do you mean?” And I realize they don’t know how I got from the first thing to the last thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I don’t mean to wander off like that. I really am trying to make a point, but sometimes I end up going through all these side paths before I can get there, and then I’m not even sure I’ve gotten there at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to keep it in order, I really do, but it’s like the order makes sense inside my head and then it doesn’t come out that way.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, like maybe I’m explaining a letter that came in the mail, or something from church, and I think I’m being clear. But then there’s some detail that reminds me of something else, like the colors in a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were sitting outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning. And then I follow that thought because it feels important, and before I know it I’m not talking about the mail anymore.\n\nThen I look at the person’s face and I can tell they’ve lost me. Or I’ve lost them, I guess. They’re waiting for me to get back to the point, but by then I’m not always sure how to get back neatly. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something, or is this something that has developed over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can put it neatly on a date like that. I don’t remember always being this way, not in the same way, but I also don’t want to say for sure exactly when it changed.\n\nWhat I notice now is that when I try to explain something, I’ll start with one clear thing, like the mail or something that happened at church, and then I get pulled into all these other pieces. I’ll mention a quilt pattern because it seems connected somehow, or the birds outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning, and while I’m talking it feels like I’m following the thread.\n\nBut then I can see the other person’s face, and they don’t know what I’m getting at. They’ll look confused, or they’ll ask me to go back, and then I realize I’ve gone somewhere else entirely. So whether it started exactly eleven months ago, I’m not sure, but it’s been much more of a problem lately, or at least I’m noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something to someone?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… at least I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a little more than some people, and I can get caught up in details, but this thing where I’m trying to explain one simple thing and it sort of wanders all over, that feels more noticeable now.\n\nLike I’ll start out telling someone about the mail I got, or something from church, and I really do think I’m staying with the point. But then something in it reminds me of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were outside, or some feeling like there was a warning in it, and I follow that because it seems connected at the time. Then after a while I can see on the person’s face that they don’t know what I’m getting at anymore.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve always had that trouble in the same way. It’s just… when it happens now, it gets away from me before I notice.\n\nDoctor: Did you used to be able to explain things clearly to others without getting sidetracked before these feelings of being watched started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I did better before. I mean, I was never a perfect storyteller or anything, I could always take a long way around a point, but I used to be able to explain normal things and have people follow me.\n\nNow it’s different. I’ll start out trying to say something simple, like about a letter that came in the mail, or something that happened at church, and then my mind catches on another piece of it. I’ll think of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were lined up outside, or something that felt like a warning, and I’ll start talking about that because in the moment it feels connected.\n\nBut then I’ll see the other person’s face, and they’re just sort of waiting, like they don’t know where I’m going with it. And then I realize I’ve gotten sidetracked and I don’t know how to get back to the beginning without sounding foolish. So yes, I used to be clearer than I am now.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something start around the same time as the feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: It does seem like it was around the same general time, yes… though I can’t say it was the exact same day or anything like that. I’m not good at lining it up cleanly.\n\nWhat I noticed is, once I started feeling watched, or maybe paying attention to things in that way, my talking got harder to keep straight. I do know that. But in the moment, it feels connected, like the bulletin and the comments and the watching are all part of the same thing. Even when I’m having a better day, I can’t completely talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times when you don't feel like you're being watched or that messages are being sent to you?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can distract myself for a little while, yes. If I’m busy with something very ordinary, like washing dishes or talking to someone about nothing important, I may not be thinking about it every minute.\n\nBut honestly, even on my better days, it’s still there in the background. That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t seem to fully shake this feeling that some people at church or in the walking club are watching me, sort of keeping track of me. And then little things feel like messages — something in the bulletin, or a comment someone makes while we’re walking — and I start thinking it was put there because of me.\n\nI know there are moments when it’s quieter in my mind, but I don’t really have days where I feel completely free of it. It’s more like I can push it aside for a bit, and then something small happens and it all comes back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to express your thoughts in a way that feels clear and understandable to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I don’t always notice it right away while I’m talking, but then I see the person’s face, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t.\n\nI’ll start off trying to explain one simple thing, like something about the mail or something that happened at church, and in my mind it all feels connected, so I keep going. But then somehow I’m talking about a quilt pattern I saw, or the birds outside my window, or how something felt like a warning, and I realize I’ve gone far away from where I started.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. I can feel the point, if that makes sense. But getting it out in a straight line is hard. I’ll be saying, “Well, the bulletin said this,” and then I remember the envelope in the mail, and then the colors on the quilt, and then the birds were all gathered in a way that seemed important, and by then the person is just looking at me like they don’t know what I’m getting at.\n\nSo yes, I think people understand pieces of what I say, but not always the whole thing. And then I get embarrassed and try to explain more, which sometimes makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think all these things - the watching, the messages, the voices - are connected in some way that makes sense to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… the watching and the messages, those feel connected to me. That’s really why I came in, because even when I’m having what I’d call a better day, when I can still go out and be polite and do the normal things, I can’t shake the feeling that certain people at church and in the walking club are watching me.\n\nIt’s not like one big thing happened all at once. It’s more little things that add up. Someone notices whether I’m there or not, or looks at me a little too long, or says something during the walk that seems casual but feels like it has another meaning. And then the church bulletin will have a phrase in it that feels too close to something about me, and I start thinking, “That was put there for me to see.”\n\nI know part of me can say maybe I’m making connections that aren’t really there. I do understand that’s possible. But it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening. It feels like the watching and the little messages are part of the same thing, like people are keeping track of me and letting me know they know. That’s the part I can’t seem to talk myself out of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I think that happens to me quite a bit, though I don’t always realize it until I’m already halfway into it.\n\nI might start out trying to tell someone something simple, like about a piece of mail I got, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it clearly. But then one thought sort of hooks onto another one. The mail makes me think of the bulletin, and then the bulletin makes me think of a quilt pattern I was working on, because of the colors or the shapes, and then I remember the birds outside and how they were gathered in a strange way, and before I know it I’m talking about that instead.\n\nIn my head, it feels connected while I’m saying it. It feels like I’m still on the same subject, in a way. But then I look at the other person and they have that blank look, or they ask me, “Wait, what do you mean?” And I realize they don’t know how I got from the first thing to the last thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I don’t mean to wander off like that. I really am trying to make a point, but sometimes I end up going through all these side paths before I can get there, and then I’m not even sure I’ve gotten there at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to keep it in order, I really do, but it’s like the order makes sense inside my head and then it doesn’t come out that way.\n\nI’ll start with one thing, like maybe I’m explaining a letter that came in the mail, or something from church, and I think I’m being clear. But then there’s some detail that reminds me of something else, like the colors in a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were sitting outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning. And then I follow that thought because it feels important, and before I know it I’m not talking about the mail anymore.\n\nThen I look at the person’s face and I can tell they’ve lost me. Or I’ve lost them, I guess. They’re waiting for me to get back to the point, but by then I’m not always sure how to get back neatly. It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it comes out all tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something, or is this something that has developed over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can put it neatly on a date like that. I don’t remember always being this way, not in the same way, but I also don’t want to say for sure exactly when it changed.\n\nWhat I notice now is that when I try to explain something, I’ll start with one clear thing, like the mail or something that happened at church, and then I get pulled into all these other pieces. I’ll mention a quilt pattern because it seems connected somehow, or the birds outside, or something that felt like it might be a warning, and while I’m talking it feels like I’m following the thread.\n\nBut then I can see the other person’s face, and they don’t know what I’m getting at. They’ll look confused, or they’ll ask me to go back, and then I realize I’ve gone somewhere else entirely. So whether it started exactly eleven months ago, I’m not sure, but it’s been much more of a problem lately, or at least I’m noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something to someone?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… at least I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a little more than some people, and I can get caught up in details, but this thing where I’m trying to explain one simple thing and it sort of wanders all over, that feels more noticeable now.\n\nLike I’ll start out telling someone about the mail I got, or something from church, and I really do think I’m staying with the point. But then something in it reminds me of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were outside, or some feeling like there was a warning in it, and I follow that because it seems connected at the time. Then after a while I can see on the person’s face that they don’t know what I’m getting at anymore.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve always had that trouble in the same way. It’s just… when it happens now, it gets away from me before I notice.\n\nDoctor: Did you used to be able to explain things clearly to others without getting sidetracked before these feelings of being watched started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I did better before. I mean, I was never a perfect storyteller or anything, I could always take a long way around a point, but I used to be able to explain normal things and have people follow me.\n\nNow it’s different. I’ll start out trying to say something simple, like about a letter that came in the mail, or something that happened at church, and then my mind catches on another piece of it. I’ll think of a quilt pattern, or the way the birds were lined up outside, or something that felt like a warning, and I’ll start talking about that because in the moment it feels connected.\n\nBut then I’ll see the other person’s face, and they’re just sort of waiting, like they don’t know where I’m going with it. And then I realize I’ve gotten sidetracked and I don’t know how to get back to the beginning without sounding foolish. So yes, I used to be clearer than I am now.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with keeping your thoughts in order when explaining something start around the same time as the feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: It does seem like it was around the same general time, yes… though I can’t say it was the exact same day or anything like that. I’m not good at lining it up cleanly.\n\nWhat I noticed is, once I started feeling watched, or maybe paying attention to things in that way, my talking got harder to keep straight. I’ll begin with something ordinary, like trying to tell someone about the mail, or something that happened at church, and I think I’m explaining it plainly. But then one part of it will make me think of a quilt pattern, or the birds outside, or some warning I felt was in there, and I follow that because in my mind it belongs with the first thing.\n\nThen after a while I see the person looking at me like they’ve lost the point, and I realize I’ve gone too far off from where I started. Yes… yes, it can happen even when I’m not trying to talk about being watched or messages or anything like that. That’s what makes it embarrassing, really, because I might be starting with something ordinary. Like I’ll be telling someone about the mail that came, just regular mail, or something someone said at church, and I think I’m explaining it in a straight line.

But then my mind sort of catches on another piece of it. I’ll mention the envelope, and then I’m thinking about the pattern on it, and that reminds me of a quilt pattern, and then maybe I remember the birds outside the window that morning, and somehow I’m talking about that. Or I start saying there was a warning in how it all lined up, even if that wasn’t what I meant to explain at first.

And while I’m saying it, it feels like it connects. It doesn’t feel random to me right away. But then I’ll look at the other person and they have that look, like they’re trying to be polite but they don’t know what I’m getting at. Then I realize I’ve gone off somewhere else and I don’t know how to bring it back neatly. So yes, my thoughts do get hard to follow sometimes, even with ordinary things.
